14: )
15: 
16: var ErrRunExecNotSupported = errors.New("exec not supported for this kind")
17: 
18: // ParseExecOutputFormat parses the exec output format user input.
19: func ParseExecOutputFormat(s string) (string, error) {
20: 	switch strings.ToLower(strings.TrimSpace(s)) {
21: 	case clabconstants.FormatJSON:
22: 		return clabconstants.FormatJSON, nil
23: 	case clabconstants.FormatPlain, clabconstants.FormatTable:
24: 		return clabconstants.FormatPlain, nil
25: 	}
26: 	return "", fmt.Errorf("cannot parse %q as execution output format, supported output formats %q",
27: 		s, []string{clabconstants.FormatJSON, clabconstants.FormatPlain})
28: }
29: 
30: // ExecCmd represents an exec command.
31: type ExecCmd struct {
32: 	Cmd []string `json:"cmd"` // Cmd is a slice-based representation of a string command.
33: }
34: 
35: // NewExecCmdFromString creates ExecCmd for a string-based command.
36: func NewExecCmdFromString(cmd string) (*ExecCmd, error) {
37: 	result := &ExecCmd{}
38: 	if err := result.SetCmd(cmd); err != nil {
39: 		return nil, err
40: 	}
41: 	return result, nil
42: }
43: 
44: // NewExecCmdFromSlice creates ExecCmd for a command represented as a slice of strings.
45: func NewExecCmdFromSlice(cmd []string) *ExecCmd {
46: 	return &ExecCmd{
47: 		Cmd: cmd,
48: 	}
49: }
50: 
51: // Stdout type alias for a string is an artificial type
52: // to allow for custom marshaling of stdout output which can be either
53: // a valid or non valid JSON.
54: // For that reason a custom MarshalJSON method is implemented to take care of both.
55: type Stdout string
56: 
57: // MarshalJSON implements a custom marshaller for a custom Stdout type.
58: func (s Stdout) MarshalJSON() ([]byte, error) {
59: 	switch {
60: 	case json.Valid([]byte(s)):
61: 		return []byte(s), nil
62: 	default:
63: 		return json.Marshal(string(s))
64: 	}
65: }
66: 
67: // ExecResult represents a result of a command execution.
68: type ExecResult struct {
69: 	Cmd        []string `json:"cmd"`
70: 	ReturnCode int      `json:"return-code"`
71: 	Stdout     Stdout   `json:"stdout"`
72: 	Stderr     string   `json:"stderr"`
73: }
74: 
75: func NewExecResult(op *ExecCmd) *ExecResult {
76: 	er := &ExecResult{Cmd: op.GetCmd()}
77: 	return er
78: }
79: 
80: // SetCmd sets the command that is to be executed.
81: func (e *ExecCmd) SetCmd(cmd string) error {
82: 	c, err := shlex.Split(cmd)
83: 	if err != nil {
84: 		return err
85: 	}
86: 	e.Cmd = c
87: 	return nil
88: }
89: 
90: // GetCmd sets the command that is to be executed.
91: func (e *ExecCmd) GetCmd() []string {
92: 	return e.Cmd
93: }
94: 
95: // GetCmdString sets the command that is to be executed.
96: func (e *ExecCmd) GetCmdString() string {
97: 	return strings.Join(e.Cmd, " ")
98: }
99: 
100: func (e *ExecResult) String() string {
101: 	var s strings.Builder
102: 
103: 	s.WriteString(fmt.Sprintf("Cmd: %s\nReturnCode: %d", e.GetCmdString(), e.ReturnCode))
104: 
105: 	if e.Stdout != "" {
106: 		s.WriteString(fmt.Sprintf("\nStdout: %q", e.Stdout))
107: 	}
108: 	if e.Stderr != "" {
109: 		s.WriteString(fmt.Sprintf("\nStderr: %q", e.Stderr))
110: 	}
111: 
112: 	return s.String()
113: }
114: 
115: // Dump dumps execution result as a string in one of the provided formats.
116: func (e *ExecResult) Dump(format string) (string, error) {
117: 	var result string
118: 	switch format {
119: 	case clabconstants.FormatJSON:
120: 		byteData, err := json.MarshalIndent(e, "", "  ")
121: 		if err != nil {
122: 			return "", err
123: 		}
124: 		result = string(byteData)
125: 	case clabconstants.FormatPlain:
126: 		result = e.String()
127: 	}
128: 	return result, nil
129: }
130: 
131: // GetCmdString returns the initially parsed cmd as a string for e.g. log output purpose.
132: func (e *ExecResult) GetCmdString() string {
133: 	return strings.Join(e.Cmd, " ")
134: }
135: 
136: func (e *ExecResult) GetReturnCode() int {
137: 	return e.ReturnCode
138: }
139: 
140: func (e *ExecResult) SetReturnCode(rc int) {
141: 	e.ReturnCode = rc
142: }
143: 
144: func (e *ExecResult) GetStdOutString() string {
145: 	return string(e.Stdout)
146: }
147: 
148: func (e *ExecResult) GetStdErrString() string {
149: 	return e.Stderr
150: }
151: 
152: func (e *ExecResult) GetStdOutByteSlice() []byte {
153: 	return []byte(e.Stdout)
154: }
155: 
156: func (e *ExecResult) GetStdErrByteSlice() []byte {
157: 	return []byte(e.Stderr)
158: }
159: 
160: func (e *ExecResult) GetCmd() []string {
161: 	return e.Cmd
162: }
163: 
164: func (e *ExecResult) SetStdOut(data []byte) {
165: 	e.Stdout = Stdout(data)
166: }
167: 
168: func (e *ExecResult) SetStdErr(data []byte) {
169: 	e.Stderr = string(data)
170: }
171: 
172: // execEntries is a map indexed by container IDs storing lists of ExecResult.
173: type execEntries map[string][]*ExecResult
174: 
175: type execEntry struct {
176: 	cId    string
177: 	result *ExecResult
178: }
179: 
180: // ExecCollection represents a datastore for exec commands execution results.
181: type ExecCollection struct {
182: 	execEntries
183: 	orderedEntries []execEntry
184: 	m              sync.RWMutex
185: }
186: 
187: // NewExecCollection initializes the collection of exec command results.
188: func NewExecCollection() *ExecCollection {
189: 	return &ExecCollection{
190: 		execEntries: execEntries{},
191: 		m:           sync.RWMutex{},
192: 	}
193: }
194: 
195: func (ec *ExecCollection) Add(cId string, e *ExecResult) {
196: 	ec.m.Lock()
197: 	defer ec.m.Unlock()
198: 	ec.execEntries[cId] = append(ec.execEntries[cId], e)
199: 	ec.orderedEntries = append(ec.orderedEntries, execEntry{cId: cId, result: e})
200: }
201: 
202: func (ec *ExecCollection) AddAll(cId string, e []*ExecResult) {
203: 	ec.m.Lock()
204: 	defer ec.m.Unlock()
205: 	ec.execEntries[cId] = append(ec.execEntries[cId], e...)
206: 	for _, result := range e {
207: 		ec.orderedEntries = append(ec.orderedEntries, execEntry{cId: cId, result: result})
208: 	}
209: }
210: 
211: // Dump dumps the contents of ExecCollection as a string in one of the provided formats.
212: func (ec *ExecCollection) Dump(format string) (string, error) {
213: 	ec.m.RLock()
214: 	defer ec.m.RUnlock()
215: 	result := strings.Builder{}
216: 	switch format {
217: 	case clabconstants.FormatJSON:
218: 		byteData, err := json.MarshalIndent(ec.execEntries, "", "  ")
219: 		if err != nil {
220: 			return "", err
221: 		}
222: 
223: 		result.Write(byteData)
224: 	case clabconstants.FormatPlain:
225: 		printSep := false
226: 		for k, execResults := range ec.execEntries {
227: 			if len(execResults) == 0 {
228: 				// skip if there is no result
229: 				continue
230: 			}
231: 
232: 			if printSep {
233: 				result.WriteString("\n+++++++++++++++++++++++++++++\n\n")
234: 			}
235: 			// write header for entry
236: 			result.WriteString("Node: ")
237: 			result.WriteString(k)
238: 			result.WriteString("\n")
239: 			for _, er := range execResults {
240: 				// write entry
241: 				result.WriteString(er.String())
242: 			}
243: 			// starting second run, print sep
244: 			printSep = true
245: 		}
246: 	}
247: 	return result.String(), nil
248: }
249: 
250: // Log writes to the log execution results stored in ExecCollection.
251: // If execution result contains error, the error log facility is used,
252: // otherwise it is logged as INFO.
253: func (ec *ExecCollection) Log() {
254: 	ec.m.RLock()
255: 	defer ec.m.RUnlock()
256: 	for _, entry := range ec.orderedEntries {
257: 		switch {
258: 		case entry.result.GetReturnCode() != 0:
259: 			log.Error(
260: 				"Failed to execute command",
261: 				"command", entry.result.GetCmdString(),
262: 				"node", entry.cId,
263: 				"rc", entry.result.GetReturnCode(),
264: 				"stdout", entry.result.GetStdOutString(),
265: 				"stderr", entry.result.GetStdErrString(),
266: 			)
267: 		default:
268: 			log.Info(
269: 				"Executed command",
270: 				"node", entry.cId,
271: 				"command", entry.result.GetCmdString(),
272: 				"stdout", entry.result.GetStdOutString(),
273: 			)
274: 		}
275: 	}
276: }

655: // GenerateConfig generates configuration for the nodes
656: // out of the template `t` based on the node configuration and saves the result to dst.
657: // If the config file is already present in the node dir we do not regenerate the config unless
658: // EnforceStartupConfig is explicitly set to true and startup-config points to a file this will
659: // persist the changes that users make to a running config when booted from some startup config.
660: func (d *DefaultNode) GenerateConfig(dst, t string) error {
661: 	// Check for incompatible options
662: 	if d.Cfg.EnforceStartupConfig && d.Cfg.SuppressStartupConfig {
663: 		return ErrIncompatibleOptions
664: 	}
665: 	if d.Cfg.EnforceStartupConfig && d.Cfg.StartupConfig == "" {
666: 		return ErrNoStartupConfig
667: 	}
668: 
669: 	if d.Cfg.SuppressStartupConfig {
670: 		log.Info("Startup config generation suppressed", "node", d.Cfg.ShortName)
671: 		return nil
672: 	}
673: 
674: 	if !d.Cfg.EnforceStartupConfig && clabutils.FileExists(dst) {
675: 		log.Debug("Existing config found", "node", d.Cfg.ShortName, "path", dst)
676: 		return nil
677: 	} else {
678: 		log.Debug("Generating config", "node", d.Cfg.ShortName, "file", d.Cfg.StartupConfig)
679: 
680: 		cfgBuf, err := clabutils.SubstituteEnvsAndTemplateWithEnv(
681: 			strings.NewReader(t),
682: 			d.Cfg,
683: 			d.Cfg.TopologyEnv,
684: 		)
685: 		if err != nil {
686: 			return err
687: 		}
688: 		log.Debug("Generated config", "node", d.Cfg.ShortName, "content", cfgBuf.String())
689: 
690: 		f, err := os.Create(dst)
691: 		if err != nil {
692: 			return err
693: 		}

40: // Deploy converges the lab to the requested topology. A lab without runtime state is
41: // deployed from scratch, an already deployed lab is reconciled in place.
42: func (c *CLab) Deploy(
43: 	ctx context.Context,
44: 	options *DeployOptions,
45: ) (*DeployResult, error) {
46: 	if options == nil {
47: 		var err error
48: 		options, err = NewDeployOptions(0)
49: 		if err != nil {
50: 			return nil, err
51: 		}
52: 	}
53: 
54: 	if options.reconfigure {
55: 		if options.dryRun {
56: 			return nil, fmt.Errorf(
57: 				"dry-run cannot be combined with reconfigure: " +
58: 					"reconfigure always destroys and redeploys the full lab",
59: 			)
60: 		}
61: 
62: 		containers, err := c.deploy(ctx, options)
63: 		if err != nil {
64: 			return nil, err
65: 		}
66: 
67: 		return &DeployResult{Containers: containers}, nil
68: 	}
69: 
70: 	if c.LabRuntime != nil && options.dryRun {
71: 		planner, ok := c.LabRuntime.(clablabruntime.TopologyPlanner)
72: 		if !ok {
73: 			return nil, fmt.Errorf("lab runtime %q does not support dry-run", c.globalRuntimeName)
74: 		}
75: 
76: 		req, err := c.labRuntimeDeployRequest()
77: 		if err != nil {
78: 			return nil, err
79: 		}
80: 		applyLabRuntimeDeployOptions(&req, options)
81: 
82: 		plan, err := planner.Plan(ctx, req)
83: 		if err != nil {
84: 			return nil, err

step-start
{
  "type": "step-start",
  "id": "prt_1cb376c3b0011AmA2vvuqcXpii",
  "sessionID": "ses_e34cb3409ffeI2aIlmPsj5N5yo",
  "messageID": "msg_1cb37655c001PA3Ml5tmHiKn8N"
}
Reasoning
Key: with `--reconfigure`, containerlab removes the whole topology lab directory (`os.RemoveAll(c.TopoPaths.TopologyLabDir())`) and then regenerates everything, including frr.conf from startup-config. So the default-case hint "It is the lab's copy of %s, which later deploys keep: fix that file and deploy again with --reconfigure" is self-contradictory: if you fix the lab directory copy and then run `--reconfigure`, the lab directory is deleted and the config regenerated from `startup-config` — the fix would be lost and the error would reappear (unless the user also fixed the startup-config).

Wait, let me re-read the hint text carefully:

```go
default:
    return fmt.Sprintf("It is the lab's copy of %s, which later deploys keep: "+
        "fix that file and deploy again with --reconfigure.", n.Cfg.StartupConfig)
```

Hmm — "It is the lab's copy of /topo/r1.conf, which later deploys keep: fix that file and deploy again with --reconfigure."

Ambiguous antecedent: "that file" — could be read as the startup-config `/topo/r1.conf`. Actually re-reading: "It is the lab's copy of X. That copy is kept by later deploys [so editing X alone won't reach the node]: fix that file [X] and deploy again with --reconfigure [which regenerates the lab copy from X]."

Under that reading: fix the startup-config source file, then `--reconfigure` regenerates the lab copy from the (fixed) startup-config, and the fix reaches the node. That's actually coherent! The doc section says the same: "The file checked is the lab directory's copy, which is the one the node uses. Created from startup-config on the first deploy, it is kept by later deploys unless enforce-startup-config is set, which is why a fixed startup-config needs --reconfigure to reach the node."

Hmm wait — but if the lab copy is kept by later deploys (GenerateConfig returns early when dst exists), how does fixing the startup-config and running `--reconfigure` reach the node? Because --reconfigure deletes the lab dir, so GenerateConfig regenerates frr.conf from the (fixed) startup-config. Yes coherent. OK so the hint is fine.

But then the doc statement "which is why a fixed startup-config needs --reconfigure to reach the node" — correct given --reconfigure removes the lab dir.

And the doc example error message shows exactly the default hint. OK.

Now the no-startup-config case hint: "Fix it, then destroy the lab without --cleanup and deploy again, which keeps the file." So for a node without startup-config, frr.conf in the lab dir is the generated default; user edits the lab copy; `destroy` (without --cleanup) keeps the lab dir; then `deploy` sees existing frr.conf (GenerateConfig keeps it). Correct.

Wait — but does plain `deploy` (no --reconfigure) on a deployed lab run PostDeploy/checkConfig again? Yes, reconciliation. And containers are recreated? On redeploy of existing lab, containers may be reused. Either way, fine.

Now, is there a concern that the check runs `vtysh --dryrun` on the container's `/etc/frr/frr.conf` — but the container's /etc/frr is bind-mounted from lab dir; `--inputfile /etc/frr/frr.conf` reads it. Good — the file the node uses.

Now let me think about vtysh --dryrun behavior more concretely. In FRR's vtysh, `--dryrun` is `-C`: "Dry run. Parse the config file and print the errors, but do not apply". Actually let me recall FRR vtysh main:

```c
case 'C':
    dryrun = 1;
    break;
```
and later:
```c
if (dryrun) {
    vtysh_read_config(config_file);  // or inputfile
    exit(0)?  
}
```

Hmm. I recall in vtysh_main.c:

```c
  if (dryrun)
    {
      if (inputfile)
        vtysh_read_config(inputfile);
      else
        vtysh_read_config(frr_config);
      exit(0);
    }
```

If it always exits 0, the check would never trigger. But newer FRR versions changed this: FRR commit "vtysh: dryrun should return non-zero on error"? Let me think. There's FRR PR #12146? I genuinely recall a change where vtysh returns EXIT_FAILURE when the config had errors in dryrun mode — added around FRR 10.x (2024). Actually yes: FRR 10.1 changelog mentions "vtysh: Return an error code when dryrun fails" or similar. There's `static int dryrun_errors` or `error_occurred`. I believe modern FRR (>= 10) returns non-zero on parse errors in dryrun. Given the containerlab image is FRR 10.7.1+, that behavior holds for the supported image. But the kind also runs plain release images of any version (docs mention plain `<version>` tags usable). On older FRR (< the version that added nonzero exit), `--dryrun` would exit 0 despite parse errors, silently disabling the check. That's a compatibility risk worth flagging as P3/P4? I can't verify which FRR version added it from the snapshot. It's an honest coverage note: "the nonzero exit behavior of vtysh --dryrun is assumed; unit tests mock the runtime; verify against the target image, and note behavior differs for older FRR images."

Actually wait — I should double check whether vtysh's error lines in dryrun mode even reach stdout/stderr as "line N: % Unknown command". The "% Unknown command" lines are emitted by vtysh when it processes config lines and the daemon rejects them... In dryrun, vtysh config parsing calls `vtysh_config_parse_line` which stores lines; the "% Unknown command[21]:" message format comes from FRR's `command.c` `vty_out(vty, "%% Unknown command: %%s")`. The `line %d:` prefix — in FRR, config file errors during daemon startup are logged by zebra etc. as "line 5: % Unknown command". Hmm, actually the "line N:" prefix is emitted by FRR's `config_from_file` in `vtysh/vtysh_config.c`: when a line fails to parse, it prints "% Unknown command[%d]"? ```

```c
static void vtysh_config_parse_line(...) {
  ...

}
```

8. Schema check: no new keys. Nothing to do.

9. Tests: ponytail requires ONE runnable check for non-trivial logic — present (several unit tests). Good.

Now — one more important behavioral check: PostDeploy is also invoked during `deploy` for nodes already running? And on `containerlab restart`? If PostDeploy runs on a *redeploy* where the node was already configured and running fine, checkConfig runs again — cheap.

But here's a real scenario issue: what about nodes deployed with the *plain* FRR release image (no containerlab flavour)? The frr kind docs say the kind works with any image? Let's see: docs say "The plain `<version>` tags ship no SSH server. Use `docker exec`..." — so the kind supports plain images. With a plain image:
- addSSHKeys: only if sshPubKeys present; keys are always collected from host? `n.sshPubKeys = params.SSHPubKeys` — containerlab always collects host pubkeys. So addSSHKeys runs the script; `id -u "$user" || continue` guards make it exit 0 on a plain image. Good.
- setPassword: guarded by credentials being set; default credentials admin/admin? defaultCredentials has Username "admin"? Actually `n.Cfg.Credentials` — where is defaultCredentials applied? In registration attributes; the framework fills Cfg.Credentials with default if unset. Then setPassword would try to chpasswd admin on a plain image — guard `id -u "$1" || exit 0` handles missing admin. Good.
- installConfigSaveWrapper: hard link /usr/bin/vtysh → /usr/bin/vtysh.containerlab; exists in plain images. Works (root exec). OK.
- checkConfig: vtysh exists in plain images. `vtysh --dryrun` exists in all modern FRR. OK.

So plain images work.

Another edge: `vtysh --dryrun` when frr.conf contains a `hostname` line etc. fine.

Now, what about `PostDeploy` being called with the node not yet started? PostDeploy runs after container start. OK.

What about `checkConfig` failing the deploy *after* the container has started — but does the deploy mark the node as failed and leave it running? Yes per design; doc says so.

Now — what about `exec` return code semantics for the wrapper: `vtysh` wrapper `exec`s the binary for non -w args; rc propagates.

One more potential real issue: **the check runs `vtysh` via RunExec with the container name — but `vtysh --dryrun` output includes lines for BOTH frr.conf parse errors and... the `% Can't open configuration file` noise is on stderr? The test filters. Fine.

Another: The error message quotes `confPath` = lab dir path, which on the user's machine is meaningful. Good.

Now — a potentially significant correctness question: **Does `vtysh --dryrun` fail (nonzero) on parse errors in all FRR versions, and does the "line N:" format match?** Covered as coverage note.

Another angle: **`strings.SplitSeq`** requires Go ≥1.24. Check go.mod.

Default case (startup-config set, not enforced):
- Lab copy exists (regenerated only if missing, since GenerateConfig returns early if file exists).

Two readings:
(a) "that file" = the lab's copy: fix the lab copy, then --reconfigure. But --reconfigure deletes the lab dir → the fix is LOST and regenerated from the (still broken) startup-config → same error again. This reading gives a broken instruction.
(b) "that file" = the startup-config source: fix startup-config, then --reconfigure regenerates the lab copy from it. Coherent.

The sentence structure "It is the lab's copy of X, which later deploys keep: fix that file..." — "that file" most naturally refers to... hmm. "which later deploys keep" is a relative clause on "the lab's copy". Then "fix that file" — ambiguous. If the intent were "fix the startup-config", the sentence would be clearer as "fix <startup-config path> and deploy again with --reconfigure". The doc section clarifies: "which is why a fixed startup-config needs --reconfigure to reach the node" — clearly intent: fix the startup-config, then --reconfigure.

Also note the doc says a fixed lab-copy (for no-startup-config nodes) requires "destroy without --cleanup". So for the default case, the hint intends: fix the *startup-config source*, and use --reconfigure so the lab copy gets regenerated. But hold on — for the default case, could the user instead just fix the lab copy and run plain `deploy` (no reconfigure)? The lab copy is kept, the fix reaches the node (bind mount). PostDeploy check would pass. So actually "fix the lab copy and deploy again" would ALSO work without --reconfigure! Hmm, would it? On plain deploy of an already-deployed lab: containers exist; does the running container see the fixed file? The bind mount is the directory, so yes, /etc/frr/frr.conf inside the container is the fixed lab copy. But the running FRR daemons already processed the old config; FRR would only re-read on restart. But the deploy check passes and the node remains with old (broken) running state until restart. Hmm — actually the node is already running with the broken config applied (minus skipped lines). Fixing the lab copy alone doesn't fix the running node until a restart/recreate. With --reconfigure, containers are recreated and read the regenerated config. So --reconfigure is the right advice... but with --reconfigure the lab copy is regenerated from startup-config — which is why the hint tells you to fix... "that file".

Given the ambiguity, if the user fixes the lab copy and runs --reconfigure, the fix is wiped. So the hint must mean the startup-config. It names the startup-config path in the sentence ("the lab's copy of %s"). It's genuinely ambiguous prose — "fix that file" after emphasizing "It is the lab's copy". Hmm. If "that file" = the lab copy, then instruction = fix lab copy + --reconfigure → wiped. If = startup-config → correct. In vtysh_main.c, the daemon connection (`vtysh_connect_all`) happens BEFORE the dryrun branch? Let me recall the order in vtysh_main:

```c
	/* Connect to the daemons. */
	if (!dryrun)
		vtysh_connect_all(...)?
```

Hmm. Actually I believe in FRR source:

```c
	if (dryrun)
		{
		...
		vtysh_read_config(inputfile?);
		exit(0);
		}
	vtysh_connect_all(...)
```

I genuinely believe dryrun short-circuits before connecting; the docstring asserts "needs no running daemons". If vtysh DID try to connect during dryrun with a timeout, it would just fail to connect and continue. Either way not a hang risk of significance.

10. **`GetStdOutString()` for large output** — fine.

Now, is there anything in the schema domain changed? No new topology keys. OK.

Docs: check that the doc's statement "the deploy checks each node's frr.conf with `vtysh --dryrun`" — accurate. And that the check happens on every `deploy`, including the initial one and reconciles. Fine.

One more doc-level check: The new section sits between "Startup configuration" and "Daemons". Fine.

Also the doc example says the error names `clab-frr01/router1/config/frr.conf` — full lab dir path. In reality LabDir is like `/root/containerlab/frr01/clab-frr01/router1` — hmm, actually LabDir is an absolute path including the CWD? In containerlab, `LabDir` = `filepath.Join(workdir, labName, shortName)`? Let me check: TopoPaths.NodeLabDir — typically `<cwd>/clab-<name>/<node>`. So the error path would be an absolute path like `/root/lab/clab-frr01/router1/config/frr.conf`, not the relative `clab-frr01/router1/config/frr.conf` shown in the doc example. The doc example is abbreviated/idealized — it shows `clab-frr01/router1/config/frr.conf`. Minor doc fidelity: real output shows an absolute path. Is that worth flagging? The doc example is illustrative; other docs show absolute paths? It's a nit; the example simplifies the path. I'd consider it too minor. Hmm, but misleading? A user comparing output might momentarily not match. Very minor. Skip or P4? I'll skip — docs commonly elide; not a defect.

Now, are there any *real* P2/P3 findings? Let me think about `configCheckErrors`'s fallback: prints ALL output including possibly huge output. vtysh dryrun output is bounded by config size — fine.

What about **the check interacting with `--skip-post-deploy`** — skipped entirely. Fine.

**Another important one**: PostDeploy is also called during `clab deploy` when only *some* nodes are targeted (node-filter)? postDeployApplyNodes runs for filtered nodes — checkConfig runs per node anyway. Fine.

**Edge: node deployed but exec fails because container crashed at startup due to broken config?** FRR never exits on bad config; daemons skip lines. So container runs. But suppose the container exits (e.g., image without vtysh — plain image has vtysh; all FRR images have vtysh). OK.

**What about non-FRR images used with kind frr?** E.g., user sets kind: frr with an arbitrary image lacking vtysh → installConfigSaveWrapper would fail earlier anyway (hard link /usr/bin/vtysh fails) → deploy already fails before check. Consistent.

Now — wait, one more scenario for the check: **`frr.conf` exists in lab dir but container uses `--dryrun` reading `/etc/frr/frr.conf`** — the bind mount maps lab config dir to /etc/frr, so identical content. But could the container's /etc/frr/frr.conf differ from the lab copy? Only if the user did `write memory` (SaveConfig writes back to lab dir too). They match. Fine.

Reasoning
No pre-existing generic config-check mechanism — the kind-specific check is the simplest correct approach.

Wait — one thing about `TestPostDeployFailsOnBadConfig` and the wrapper install: the mock executes bash? No, it's mocked. Good.

Now one more consideration about checkConfig and **vtysh dryrun error detection when FRR *succeeds* in parsing but prints warnings with "line N:" prefix?** E.g., "% Warning" lines don't have "line N:" prefix. And vtysh exits 0. Only matters if rc!=0.

Another: **stdout+stderr merged then filtered — if rc != 0 but only non-"line" lines present, fallback prints everything.** OK.

Now think about whether the check should be skipped for `--dry-run` deploys: dry-run doesn't create containers, so PostDeploy doesn't run at all in dry-run. Fine.

Now — one more angle: **`checkConfig` failing blocks `containerlab deploy` but what about `containerlab start` or `restart`?** Those don't call PostDeploy (restart restarts containers). So a broken config reaches the node via restart without a check. Fine — consistent scope.

Now finalize the coverage of the three changed paths:
1. docs/manual/kinds/frr.md — reviewed; claims match code; anchors valid.
2. nodes/frr/frr.go — reviewed; callers traced (PostDeploy, RunExec semantics, deploy error aggregation, GenerateConfig, --reconfigure semantics).
3. nodes/frr/frr_test.go — reviewed; helpers exist; Times(2) consistent.

Also schema check: no schema domain changes (no new topology keys; `daemons` unchanged). Confirmed no schema work needed.

Now — decide findings list:

Finding 1 (P4): `configFixHint` default-case wording is ambiguous; "fix that file" could be read as the lab-directory copy, and `--reconfigure` deletes the lab directory (`os.RemoveAll(c.TopoPaths.TopologyLabDir())` in core/deploy.go), regenerating frr.conf from the startup-config — a user who edits the lab copy per the literal reading loses the fix and hits the same error. Suggest rewording to name the startup-config explicitly, e.g. "Fix <startup-config> and deploy again with --reconfigure, which regenerates the lab copy."

Hmm wait — actually, is this really ambiguous enough to report? Let me weigh once more. The sentence: "It is the lab's copy of router1/frr.conf, which later deploys keep: fix that file and deploy again with --reconfigure."

The colon clause follows the assertion about the lab copy. "fix that file" — "that file" — the last definite referent is "the lab's copy". A user told "It is the lab's copy of X... fix that file" would most likely open the lab copy. Then `--reconfigure` wipes it. That's a genuine trap with real user cost (fix lost). And the correct workflow is non-obvious. Yes, report as P4 (or P3?). Impact: user follows instruction, fix silently lost on redeploy, error repeats. No data loss beyond their edit to a generated file. I'd say P4 optional improvement (clarity). Actually the misleading instruction is actionable and concrete — P3? The definitions: P2 correctness/reliability defect; P3 minor defect. The error message gives a wrong-sounding instruction under one reading — a minor UX defect in the error path. Hmm. But the doc clarifies. And arguably the intended reading is fine. I'll go P4 and word it as an ambiguity with a concrete suggested rewording. Hmm, actually let me reconsider: if the user fixes the lab copy and redeploys with --reconfigure, the config is regenerated from the broken startup-config → same failure → user confused, loses edit. That's a real reliability cost of following the message. But the message can also be read correctly. P4 feels right; it's a clarity defect with a cheap fix.

Finding 2 (P4?): `configCheckErrors` returns `"  "` (a line of two spaces) when vtysh exits nonzero with no output, producing an empty indented line in the error between the header and the hint. Trigger: nonzero rc with empty stdout and stderr. Impact: cosmetic; slightly confusing error. Fix: return "" when out is empty. Is this worth a finding? It's very minor... I'll include it as P4 since it's concrete and has a one-line correction. Hmm — actually, wait: is the trigger realistic? vtysh failing with zero output: e.g., OOM-killed (rc 137), or a vtysh crash. Rare but possible. The error would then be "…unconfigured:\n  \nFix it…" — nearly unreadable (no cause shown). Actually the deeper issue: with no output, the error tells the user nothing about what failed — the fallback is fine for unstructured output, but empty output yields no cause at all. Minor. I'll include as P4.

Hmm, actually, let me reconsider whether to include it at all. "Return only supported, useful findings." A two-space line... The error becomes:

The snapshot review is complete. Final summary below.

## Review### Summary
The PR adds a new post-deploy configuration check for the FRR kind: after the config-save wrapper is installed, `PostDeploy` runs `vtysh --dryrun` against the node's rendered `/etc/frr/frr.conf` and fails the deploy when vtysh rejects the file. The error names the offending lines (via the `line N:` output format), explains why FRR still runs with those lines skipped, and gives a fix workflow tailored to whether a `startup-config` exists. Documentation is updated in the FRR kind guide. The change is well-scoped: it is FRR-specific (no generic hook invented), ordered last in `PostDeploy` so a failing node stays reachable with credentials and SSH keys in place, and the wrapper installed just before it passes `--dryrun` through to the real `vtysh` binary.

I traced the supporting machinery and the documented claims hold up: `RunExec` returns nil for nonzero exit codes (so the return-code handling in `checkConfig` is correct), the deployment error chain (`waitForNodeDeploy` + `postDeployApplyNodes`) produces exactly the text shown in the doc example, and the `--reconfigure` / `destroy` semantics match the three fix-hint variants. No topology schema keys were added or changed, so no schema work is needed. Two minor, optional-quality findings remain.

### Findings table

| Priority | Finding | Location |
|---|---|---|
| P4 | `configCheckErrors` emits a stray two-space line when vtysh fails with no output | [nodes/frr/frr.go#L324-L326](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/d98a9dfaf3d76bb54c234a7f23a09ec1a5d5d2b4/nodes/frr/frr.go#L324-L326) |
| P4 | `configFixHint` default-case wording can point users at the lab copy, which `--reconfigure` overwrites | [nodes/frr/frr.go#L342-L345](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/d98a9dfaf3d76bb54c234a7f23a09ec1a5d5d2b4/nodes/frr/frr.go#L342-L345) |

### Finding details

#### P4 — `configCheckErrors` emits a stray two-space line when vtysh fails with no output
**Location:** [nodes/frr/frr.go#L324-L326](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/d98a9dfaf3d76bb54c234a7f23a09ec1a5d5d2b4/nodes/frr/frr.go#L324-L326)
**Problem:** When `vtysh --dryrun` exits nonzero with completely empty stdout and stderr, the fallback branch returns `"  "` (the two-space indent with nothing after it). The resulting error renders as the "has errors that FRR skips at startup" header, an empty indented line, and then the fix hint — with no cause at all between them.
**Trigger:** A nonzero exit with no output, for example a vtysh crash or the exec being killed (e.g., exit 137), on any `frr` node during `deploy`.
**Impact:** Cosmetic confusion; the user gets an error message with an empty line and no explanation of what failed.
**Correction:** In the `out == ""` case return an empty string (or a short "vtysh exited with code %d without output" line) so the message stays clean.

#### P4 — `configFixHint` default-case wording can point users at the lab copy, which `--reconfigure` overwrites
**Location:** [nodes/frr/frr.go#L342-L345](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/d98a9dfaf3d76bb54c234a7f23a09ec1a5d5d2b4/nodes/frr/frr.go#L342-L345)
**Problem:** The default hint reads "It is the lab's copy of `<startup-config>`, which later deploys keep: fix that file and deploy again with `--reconfigure`." The most natural referent of "that file" is the lab's copy just emphasized in the previous clause. But `--reconfigure` deletes the entire lab directory (`os.RemoveAll(TopologyLabDir())` in `core/deploy.go`) and regenerates `frr.conf` from the `startup-config` source, so a user who follows the literal reading loses their edit and hits the same error. The intended workflow (fix the `startup-config`, then `--reconfigure`) is stated correctly in the new [Configuration check docs section](https://github.com/srl-labs/containerlab/blob/d98a9dfaf3d76bb54c234a7f23a09ec1a5d5d2b4/docs/manual/kinds/frr.md#L104-L124), but the error message stands alone in deploy output.
**Trigger:** Any `frr` node with a `startup-config` whose rendered config fails `vtysh --dryrun`; the user follows the hint as literally written.
**Impact:** A wasted editing round and a puzzling repeat of the same failure; no data loss beyond an edit to a generated file.
**Correction:** Name the startup-config explicitly in the hint, e.g. "Fix `<startup-config>` and deploy again with `--reconfigure`, which regenerates the lab copy from it." The enforce and no-startup-config variants are unambiguous and need no change.

### Coverage notes
- All three changed paths were reviewed against the base revision; the diff is entirely the new FRR config-check feature (`checkConfig`, `configCheckErrors`, `configFixHint`, tests, docs section).
- Verified the error aggregation chain matches the doc example: `core/deploy.go` `waitForNodeDeploy` ("deployment failed for one or more nodes: %w") plus `postDeployApplyNodes`/`core/clab.go` ("node %q post-deploy: %w").
- Verified `RunExec` (`nodes/default_node.go`) returns nil on nonzero exit codes and reports them via `ExecResult.GetReturnCode()`, so `checkConfig`'s return-code handling is correct; the passing-shell-command false-positive concern in the earlier step is resolved.
- Verified the config-save wrapper passes non-`-w`/`--writeconfig` invocations (including `--dryrun`) to the real `vtysh`, so the check is not affected by the wrapper.
- Verified the fix workflows against `GenerateConfig`, `--reconfigure`, and `redeploy` (destroy without `--cleanup`) semantics; the doc's claims about when the lab copy is kept or regenerated are accurate.
- Unit tests cover the hint variants, rc handling, line filtering, empty-output fallback, and `PostDeploy` ordering (the `.Times(2)` `RunExec` expectation is consistent with credential-less nodes skipping `addSSHKeys`/`setPassword`). The in-repo Robot suite (`tests/14-basic-frr/01-frr.robot`) deploys valid configs, so the check runs in CI, but no e2e case exercises a *failing* config.
- I could not verify the real `vtysh --dryrun` exit-code and `line N:` output-format behavior from the snapshot (the mocks are the only source); this assumption should be confirmed against the pinned FRR image in a real CI run. Older plain-FRR images would at worst make the check a no-op (exit 0), not a false positive.
- No topology schema keys were added or changed (`startup-config`, `enforce-startup-config`, `suppress-startup-config`, `daemons` all pre-exist), so no `clab.schema.json` work is required.
- No `docs/rn/` changes in the diff, so the release-note review rule does not apply. No CI formatting failures were supplied; the code appears gofmt-consistent.
- Source-only review: no tests or container runs were executed by me; findings above are from static analysis.
